Yeah, um… I’m here because work’s gotten kind of hard to deal with. I’m a construction manager, so I’m around job sites all the time, and anytime I have to get near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or one of those upper platforms, my head just starts telling me I’m about to go over.

And the thing is, I can see the rails are there. I know the setup’s supposed to be safe — I mean, I’ve checked those things myself plenty of times. I know how it’s built. But it doesn’t seem to matter. I get up there and my mind just goes straight to, “You’re going over.” It’s… it’s been getting to where I don’t know how I’m supposed to do my job like this.

Yeah, I mean… the big thing is definitely on job sites. Like when I’m near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or one of those upper platforms, it just hits me — my head tells me I’m about to go over. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… yeah, it’s the same kind of thing, but what’s really been happening is at work. Every time I have to get near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or one of those upper platforms, my head just goes straight to, like, “you’re going to go over.”

And I know that probably sounds strange because I can see the rails are there. I know they’re supposed to be safe — I’ve inspected that stuff myself. I’ve been doing this long enough to know what’s solid and what isn’t. But it doesn’t matter much in the moment. It just hits me like I’m about to fall, even when nothing’s actually wrong.

With family or friends… I guess if it’s a high place, I’d be thinking about it, but the main thing is the job sites. That’s where it’s been hard to get around.

Yeah… I mean, I guess if I’m up high anywhere, my mind can go there, but the main place it really shows up is work. Like, every time I have to get near a scaffold or a roof edge or one of those upper platforms, it’s like my head just starts yelling that I’m about to go over.

And I know that sounds ridiculous because I can see the rails right there. I know the setup is safe — or supposed to be safe — I mean, I’ve been the guy checking that kind of thing. It’s funny, not funny really, but I can tell a crew the platform’s secure and then my own brain won’t believe it when I’m the one standing there. It just… doesn’t matter what I know in that moment.

Honestly, I’m trying to think of a time where it doesn’t happen… and I’m kind of coming up blank. Like, even if it’s not work exactly, the second I step onto temporary stairs or even just walk toward an open upper floor, my chest tightens up. It’s weird because I’ll be telling myself, “Just move, it’s fine,” but my body’s already reacting. And then I start thinking about dumb stuff, like how I used to run up stairs without even thinking about it, or how other guys on site just walk around like nothing’s happening. But for me, once I’m up there or heading toward that edge, it just kicks in.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I’m here because work’s gotten kind of hard to deal with. I’m a construction manager, so I’m around job sites all the time, and anytime I have to get near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or one of those upper platforms, my head just starts telling me I’m about to go over.\n\nAnd the thing is, I can see the rails are there. I know the setup’s supposed to be safe — I mean, I’ve checked those things myself plenty of times. I know how it’s built. But it doesn’t seem to matter. I get up there and my mind just goes straight to, “You’re going over.” It’s… it’s been getting to where I don’t know how I’m supposed to do my job like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this fear of falling when you're in other high places outside of work, like a balcony or a mountain overlook?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the big thing is definitely on job sites. Like when I’m near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or one of those upper platforms, it just hits me — my head tells me I’m about to go over. And I can be looking right at the rails, knowing they’re in place, knowing the setup is supposed to be safe because that’s literally part of my job to understand… but it doesn’t really calm me down.\n\nI don’t know, balconies and that kind of thing… I guess I think of the same sort of feeling, but work is where it’s been really in my face. Like I’ll be standing there trying to focus on the crew or the plan for the day, and suddenly all I can think is, “I’m going to fall.”\n\nDoctor: Does this fear of falling happen to you when you're not working, like when you're with family or friends in a high place?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, it’s the same kind of thing, but what’s really been happening is at work. Every time I have to get near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or one of those upper platforms, my head just goes straight to, like, “you’re going to go over.”\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds strange because I can see the rails are there. I know they’re supposed to be safe — I’ve inspected that stuff myself. I’ve been doing this long enough to know what’s solid and what isn’t. But it doesn’t matter much in the moment. It just hits me like I’m about to fall, even when nothing’s actually wrong.\n\nWith family or friends… I guess if it’s a high place, I’d be thinking about it, but the main thing is the job sites. That’s where it’s been hard to get around.\n\nDoctor: Is this fear of falling ever present when you are in high places and not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess if I’m up high anywhere, my mind can go there, but the main place it really shows up is work. Like, every time I have to get near a scaffold or a roof edge or one of those upper platforms, it’s like my head just starts yelling that I’m about to go over.\n\nAnd I know that sounds ridiculous because I can see the rails right there. I know the setup is safe — or supposed to be safe — I mean, I’ve been the guy checking that kind of thing. It’s funny, not funny really, but I can tell a crew the platform’s secure and then my own brain won’t believe it when I’m the one standing there. It just… doesn’t matter what I know in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you're in a high place, not thinking about work, and you don't experience this fear of falling?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m trying to think of a time where it doesn’t happen… and I’m kind of coming up blank. Like, even if it’s not work exactly, the second I step onto temporary stairs or even just walk toward an open upper floor, my chest tightens up. My hands get wet, and I feel like my legs might lock before I can make myself take another step.\n\nIt’s weird because I’ll be telling myself, “Just move, it’s fine,” but my body’s already reacting. And then I start thinking about dumb stuff, like how I used to run up stairs without even thinking about it, or how other guys on site just walk around like nothing’s happening. But for me, once I’m up there or heading toward that edge, it just kicks in.\n\nDoctor: Is this fear of falling from heights something that you've experienced at any point in your life before you started working in construction?\n\nPatient: No… not really before that. I mean, I wasn’t someone who loved heights or anything, but I could do the job. I could be up where I needed to be.\n\nThis really started after the stair tower accident, about… thirteen months ago now. Funny how I can remember that so clearly, but I’ll forget where I put my keys half the time. But yeah, before that accident, this wasn’t like this.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of falling something that you experience in your dreams or when you're not actively thinking about work or being in high places?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, dreams are hard for me to pin down—half the time I wake up and it’s gone, like when you walk into a room and forget why you went in there.\n\nWhen I’m not thinking about work or being up high, I don’t know that I’ve noticed it showing up on its own. It’s more like… I only really recognize it when something puts heights in my head. But I haven’t exactly been keeping track, you know?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah… I mean, outside of work-work, it’s mostly the daily stuff around the job, if that makes sense. Like I’ll put off an upper-level walk-through, or I’ll send somebody else up first, and then suddenly everyone’s looking at me like, “Why are you making that call if you haven’t been up there yourself?”

And crews talk, you know. Subcontractors too. One guy gives you a look and then it’s like being back in high school shop class—everybody knows something’s off. I’ve delayed enough of those inspections and walk-throughs that people have started questioning me, and that’s… that’s not great in my position.

It got bad enough that my employer’s insurer pushed for an occupational health review because I wasn’t getting some of the key safety inspections done. So yeah, it’s affecting things. Maybe not like dinner with my family or going to the grocery store, but my day-to-day and relationships with people I work with, definitely.

I mean, dreams are hard for me to pin down—half the time I wake up and it’s gone, like when you walk into a room and forget why you went in there.\n\nWhen I’m not thinking about work or being up high, I don’t know that I’ve noticed it showing up on its own. It’s more like… I only really recognize it when something puts heights in my head. But I haven’t exactly been keeping track, you know?\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of falling from heights something that affects your personal relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, outside of work-work, it’s mostly the daily stuff around the job, if that makes sense. Like I’ll put off an upper-level walk-through, or I’ll send somebody else up first, and then suddenly everyone’s looking at me like, “Why are you making that call if you haven’t been up there yourself?”\n\nAnd crews talk, you know. Subcontractors too. One guy gives you a look and then it’s like being back in high school shop class—everybody knows something’s off. I’ve delayed enough of those inspections and walk-throughs that people have started questioning me, and that’s… that’s not great in my position.\n\nIt got bad enough that my employer’s insurer pushed for an occupational health review because I wasn’t getting some of the key safety inspections done. So yeah, it’s affecting things. Maybe not like dinner with my family or going to the grocery store, but my day-to-day and relationships with people I work with, definitely.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs the fear of falling from heights something that you experience when you are alone, not at work, and not thinking about job-related tasks?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 44 / construction_manager / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know… I haven’t really caught myself feeling it just sitting at home or anything like that.

Like if I’m alone, my mind usually goes to chores or bills or whatever’s broken around the house—there’s always something. Last week it was the water heater making that knocking sound, and then I got pulled into looking up parts for it for two hours. So, no, I can’t say I notice the fear just coming out of nowhere when I’m not thinking about heights or work stuff. At least not that I’m aware of.

I know the setup’s supposed to be safe — I mean, I’ve checked those things myself plenty of times. I know how it’s built. But it doesn’t seem to matter. I get up there and my mind just goes straight to, “You’re going over.” It’s… it’s been getting to where I don’t know how I’m supposed to do my job like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this fear of falling when you're in other high places outside of work, like a balcony or a mountain overlook?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the big thing is definitely on job sites. Like when I’m near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or one of those upper platforms, it just hits me — my head tells me I’m about to go over. And I can be looking right at the rails, knowing they’re in place, knowing the setup is supposed to be safe because that’s literally part of my job to understand… but it doesn’t really calm me down.\n\nI don’t know, balconies and that kind of thing… I guess I think of the same sort of feeling, but work is where it’s been really in my face. Like I’ll be standing there trying to focus on the crew or the plan for the day, and suddenly all I can think is, “I’m going to fall.”\n\nDoctor: Does this fear of falling happen to you when you're not working, like when you're with family or friends in a high place?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, it’s the same kind of thing, but what’s really been happening is at work. Every time I have to get near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or one of those upper platforms, my head just goes straight to, like, “you’re going to go over.”\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds strange because I can see the rails are there. I know they’re supposed to be safe — I’ve inspected that stuff myself. I’ve been doing this long enough to know what’s solid and what isn’t. But it doesn’t matter much in the moment. That’s where it’s been hard to get around.\n\nDoctor: Is this fear of falling ever present when you are in high places and not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess if I’m up high anywhere, my mind can go there, but the main place it really shows up is work. Like, even if it’s not work exactly, the second I step onto temporary stairs or even just walk toward an open upper floor, my chest tightens up. Like I’ll put off an upper-level walk-through, or I’ll send somebody else up first, and then suddenly everyone’s looking at me like, “Why are you making that call if you haven’t been up there yourself?”\n\nAnd crews talk, you know. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. I’m not sure I’ve got a clean example of that. Like, if there’s no work tied to it and no actual height involved… I don’t really notice it showing up.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I’m here because work’s gotten kind of hard to deal with. I’m a construction manager, so I’m around job sites all the time, and anytime I have to get near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or one of those upper platforms, my head just starts telling me I’m about to go over.\n\nAnd the thing is, I can see the rails are there. I know the setup’s supposed to be safe — I mean, I’ve checked those things myself plenty of times. I know how it’s built. But it doesn’t seem to matter. I get up there and my mind just goes straight to, “You’re going over.” It’s… it’s been getting to where I don’t know how I’m supposed to do my job like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this fear of falling when you're in other high places outside of work, like a balcony or a mountain overlook?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the big thing is definitely on job sites. Like when I’m near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or one of those upper platforms, it just hits me — my head tells me I’m about to go over. And I can be looking right at the rails, knowing they’re in place, knowing the setup is supposed to be safe because that’s literally part of my job to understand… but it doesn’t really calm me down.\n\nI don’t know, balconies and that kind of thing… I guess I think of the same sort of feeling, but work is where it’s been really in my face. Like I’ll be standing there trying to focus on the crew or the plan for the day, and suddenly all I can think is, “I’m going to fall.”\n\nDoctor: Does this fear of falling happen to you when you're not working, like when you're with family or friends in a high place?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, it’s the same kind of thing, but what’s really been happening is at work. Every time I have to get near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or one of those upper platforms, my head just goes straight to, like, “you’re going to go over.”\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds strange because I can see the rails are there. I know they’re supposed to be safe — I’ve inspected that stuff myself. I’ve been doing this long enough to know what’s solid and what isn’t. But it doesn’t matter much in the moment. It just hits me like I’m about to fall, even when nothing’s actually wrong.\n\nWith family or friends… I guess if it’s a high place, I’d be thinking about it, but the main thing is the job sites. That’s where it’s been hard to get around.\n\nDoctor: Is this fear of falling ever present when you are in high places and not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess if I’m up high anywhere, my mind can go there, but the main place it really shows up is work. Like, every time I have to get near a scaffold or a roof edge or one of those upper platforms, it’s like my head just starts yelling that I’m about to go over.\n\nAnd I know that sounds ridiculous because I can see the rails right there. I know the setup is safe — or supposed to be safe — I mean, I’ve been the guy checking that kind of thing. It’s funny, not funny really, but I can tell a crew the platform’s secure and then my own brain won’t believe it when I’m the one standing there. It just… doesn’t matter what I know in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you're in a high place, not thinking about work, and you don't experience this fear of falling?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m trying to think of a time where it doesn’t happen… and I’m kind of coming up blank. Like, even if it’s not work exactly, the second I step onto temporary stairs or even just walk toward an open upper floor, my chest tightens up. My hands get wet, and I feel like my legs might lock before I can make myself take another step.\n\nIt’s weird because I’ll be telling myself, “Just move, it’s fine,” but my body’s already reacting. And then I start thinking about dumb stuff, like how I used to run up stairs without even thinking about it, or how other guys on site just walk around like nothing’s happening. But for me, once I’m up there or heading toward that edge, it just kicks in.\n\nDoctor: Is this fear of falling from heights something that you've experienced at any point in your life before you started working in construction?\n\nPatient: No… not really before that. I mean, I wasn’t someone who loved heights or anything, but I could do the job. I could be up where I needed to be.\n\nThis really started after the stair tower accident, about… thirteen months ago now. Funny how I can remember that so clearly, but I’ll forget where I put my keys half the time. But yeah, before that accident, this wasn’t like this.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of falling something that you experience in your dreams or when you're not actively thinking about work or being in high places?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, dreams are hard for me to pin down—half the time I wake up and it’s gone, like when you walk into a room and forget why you went in there.\n\nWhen I’m not thinking about work or being up high, I don’t know that I’ve noticed it showing up on its own. It’s more like… I only really recognize it when something puts heights in my head. But I haven’t exactly been keeping track, you know?\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of falling from heights something that affects your personal relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, outside of work-work, it’s mostly the daily stuff around the job, if that makes sense. Like I’ll put off an upper-level walk-through, or I’ll send somebody else up first, and then suddenly everyone’s looking at me like, “Why are you making that call if you haven’t been up there yourself?”\n\nAnd crews talk, you know. Subcontractors too. One guy gives you a look and then it’s like being back in high school shop class—everybody knows something’s off. I’ve delayed enough of those inspections and walk-throughs that people have started questioning me, and that’s… that’s not great in my position.\n\nIt got bad enough that my employer’s insurer pushed for an occupational health review because I wasn’t getting some of the key safety inspections done. So yeah, it’s affecting things. Maybe not like dinner with my family or going to the grocery store, but my day-to-day and relationships with people I work with, definitely.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of falling from heights something that you experience when you are alone, not at work, and not thinking about job-related tasks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really caught myself feeling it just sitting at home or anything like that.\n\nLike if I’m alone, my mind usually goes to chores or bills or whatever’s broken around the house—there’s always something. Last week it was the water heater making that knocking sound, and then I got pulled into looking up parts for it for two hours. So, no, I can’t say I notice the fear just coming out of nowhere when I’m not thinking about heights or work stuff. At least not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Does this fear of falling ever occur to you in situations that have absolutely no connection to your job or work-related activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve got a clean example of that. Like, if there’s no work tied to it and no actual height involved… I don’t really notice it showing up.\n\nI mean, I’ll get bothered if something makes me think about being up high, but that’s kind of the point, right? It’s connected to heights somehow. If I’m just at home or doing normal stuff—taking the trash out, watching TV, fixing something in the garage—I can’t say it just pops into my head on its own.\n\nUnless I’m misunderstanding what you mean by “absolutely no connection.”\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If I’m just at home or doing normal stuff—taking the trash out, watching TV, fixing something in the garage—I can’t say it just pops into my head on its own.\n\nUnless I’m misunderstanding what you mean by “absolutely no connection.”\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you recall a situation where you were in a high place, not at work, and not thinking about work, and you still experienced this fear of falling?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 44 / construction_manager / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Specific_Phobic_Fear\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because every time I have to get near a scaffold, roof edge, or upper platform now, my head tells me I’m about to go over even when I can see the rails are in place and I know the setup is supposed to be safe.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I mean… if I’m being honest, it’s mostly when I’m near something like a scaffold, a roof edge, or an upper platform. That’s when it hits me.

Even if I can look right at it and see the rails are there, and I know—like logically I know—it’s supposed to be safe, my head just goes, “You’re about to go over.” It’s stupid, because I’ve spent years around that kind of setup, you know? I can tell when something’s built right. I’ve corrected guys on rail heights and tie-offs and all that.

But now when I get near the edge, work or not, my brain doesn’t care. It just tells me I’m going to fall.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I’m here because work’s gotten kind of hard to deal with. I’m a construction manager, so I’m around job sites all the time, and anytime I have to get near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or one of those upper platforms, my head just starts telling me I’m about to go over.\n\nAnd the thing is, I can see the rails are there. I know the setup’s supposed to be safe — I mean, I’ve checked those things myself plenty of times. I know how it’s built. But it doesn’t seem to matter. I get up there and my mind just goes straight to, “You’re going over.” It’s… it’s been getting to where I don’t know how I’m supposed to do my job like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this fear of falling when you're in other high places outside of work, like a balcony or a mountain overlook?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the big thing is definitely on job sites. Like when I’m near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or one of those upper platforms, it just hits me — my head tells me I’m about to go over. And I can be looking right at the rails, knowing they’re in place, knowing the setup is supposed to be safe because that’s literally part of my job to understand… but it doesn’t really calm me down.\n\nI don’t know, balconies and that kind of thing… I guess I think of the same sort of feeling, but work is where it’s been really in my face. Like I’ll be standing there trying to focus on the crew or the plan for the day, and suddenly all I can think is, “I’m going to fall.”\n\nDoctor: Does this fear of falling happen to you when you're not working, like when you're with family or friends in a high place?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, it’s the same kind of thing, but what’s really been happening is at work. Every time I have to get near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or one of those upper platforms, my head just goes straight to, like, “you’re going to go over.”\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds strange because I can see the rails are there. I know they’re supposed to be safe — I’ve inspected that stuff myself. I’ve been doing this long enough to know what’s solid and what isn’t. But it doesn’t matter much in the moment. It just hits me like I’m about to fall, even when nothing’s actually wrong.\n\nWith family or friends… I guess if it’s a high place, I’d be thinking about it, but the main thing is the job sites. That’s where it’s been hard to get around.\n\nDoctor: Is this fear of falling ever present when you are in high places and not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess if I’m up high anywhere, my mind can go there, but the main place it really shows up is work. Like, every time I have to get near a scaffold or a roof edge or one of those upper platforms, it’s like my head just starts yelling that I’m about to go over.\n\nAnd I know that sounds ridiculous because I can see the rails right there. I know the setup is safe — or supposed to be safe — I mean, I’ve been the guy checking that kind of thing. It’s funny, not funny really, but I can tell a crew the platform’s secure and then my own brain won’t believe it when I’m the one standing there. It just… doesn’t matter what I know in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you're in a high place, not thinking about work, and you don't experience this fear of falling?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m trying to think of a time where it doesn’t happen… and I’m kind of coming up blank. Like, even if it’s not work exactly, the second I step onto temporary stairs or even just walk toward an open upper floor, my chest tightens up. My hands get wet, and I feel like my legs might lock before I can make myself take another step.\n\nIt’s weird because I’ll be telling myself, “Just move, it’s fine,” but my body’s already reacting. And then I start thinking about dumb stuff, like how I used to run up stairs without even thinking about it, or how other guys on site just walk around like nothing’s happening. But for me, once I’m up there or heading toward that edge, it just kicks in.\n\nDoctor: Is this fear of falling from heights something that you've experienced at any point in your life before you started working in construction?\n\nPatient: No… not really before that. I mean, I wasn’t someone who loved heights or anything, but I could do the job. I could be up where I needed to be.\n\nThis really started after the stair tower accident, about… thirteen months ago now. Funny how I can remember that so clearly, but I’ll forget where I put my keys half the time. But yeah, before that accident, this wasn’t like this.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of falling something that you experience in your dreams or when you're not actively thinking about work or being in high places?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, dreams are hard for me to pin down—half the time I wake up and it’s gone, like when you walk into a room and forget why you went in there.\n\nWhen I’m not thinking about work or being up high, I don’t know that I’ve noticed it showing up on its own. It’s more like… I only really recognize it when something puts heights in my head. But I haven’t exactly been keeping track, you know?\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of falling from heights something that affects your personal relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, outside of work-work, it’s mostly the daily stuff around the job, if that makes sense. Like I’ll put off an upper-level walk-through, or I’ll send somebody else up first, and then suddenly everyone’s looking at me like, “Why are you making that call if you haven’t been up there yourself?”\n\nAnd crews talk, you know. Subcontractors too. One guy gives you a look and then it’s like being back in high school shop class—everybody knows something’s off. I’ve delayed enough of those inspections and walk-throughs that people have started questioning me, and that’s… that’s not great in my position.\n\nIt got bad enough that my employer’s insurer pushed for an occupational health review because I wasn’t getting some of the key safety inspections done. So yeah, it’s affecting things. Maybe not like dinner with my family or going to the grocery store, but my day-to-day and relationships with people I work with, definitely.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of falling from heights something that you experience when you are alone, not at work, and not thinking about job-related tasks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really caught myself feeling it just sitting at home or anything like that.\n\nLike if I’m alone, my mind usually goes to chores or bills or whatever’s broken around the house—there’s always something. Last week it was the water heater making that knocking sound, and then I got pulled into looking up parts for it for two hours. So, no, I can’t say I notice the fear just coming out of nowhere when I’m not thinking about heights or work stuff. At least not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Does this fear of falling ever occur to you in situations that have absolutely no connection to your job or work-related activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve got a clean example of that. Like, if there’s no work tied to it and no actual height involved… I don’t really notice it showing up.\n\nI mean, I’ll get bothered if something makes me think about being up high, but that’s kind of the point, right? It’s connected to heights somehow. If I’m just at home or doing normal stuff—taking the trash out, watching TV, fixing something in the garage—I can’t say it just pops into my head on its own.\n\nUnless I’m misunderstanding what you mean by “absolutely no connection.”\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a situation where you were in a high place, not at work, and not thinking about work, and you still experienced this fear of falling?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… if I’m being honest, it’s mostly when I’m near something like a scaffold, a roof edge, or an upper platform. That’s when it hits me.\n\nEven if I can look right at it and see the rails are there, and I know—like logically I know—it’s supposed to be safe, my head just goes, “You’re about to go over.” It’s stupid, because I’ve spent years around that kind of setup, you know? I can tell when something’s built right. I’ve corrected guys on rail heights and tie-offs and all that.\n\nBut now when I get near the edge, work or not, my brain doesn’t care. It just tells me I’m going to fall.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Is the fear of falling from heights something that you experience when you are alone, not at work, and not thinking about job-related tasks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really caught myself feeling it just sitting at home or anything like that.\n\nLike if I’m alone, my mind usually goes to chores or bills or whatever’s broken around the house—there’s always something. Last week it was the water heater making that knocking sound, and then I got pulled into looking up parts for it for two hours. So, no, I can’t say I notice the fear just coming out of nowhere when I’m not thinking about heights or work stuff. That’s when it hits me.\n\nEven if I can look right at it and see the rails are there, and I know—like logically I know—it’s supposed to be safe, my head just goes, “You’re about to go over.” It’s stupid, because I’ve spent years around that kind of setup, you know? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, the recent ones all kind of blur together because it’s the same thing every time I get near something high. Like a roof edge, or one of those upper platforms, or even just seeing a scaffold set up somewhere.

I know that sounds work-related, but it’s not always me working. It’s more like if I’m near it, my head just flips into this thing where it tells me I’m about to go over. Even if I can see the rails are there, even if I know it’s set up right and it should be safe.

It’s frustrating because I understand that stuff better than most people. I’ll be looking at it thinking, “That’s fine, that’s built right,” and then my body—or my brain, I guess—just doesn’t care. It’s like, “Nope, you’re going to fall.”

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I’m here because work’s gotten kind of hard to deal with. I’m a construction manager, so I’m around job sites all the time, and anytime I have to get near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or one of those upper platforms, my head just starts telling me I’m about to go over.\n\nAnd the thing is, I can see the rails are there. I know the setup’s supposed to be safe — I mean, I’ve checked those things myself plenty of times. I know how it’s built. But it doesn’t seem to matter. I get up there and my mind just goes straight to, “You’re going over.” It’s… it’s been getting to where I don’t know how I’m supposed to do my job like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this fear of falling when you're in other high places outside of work, like a balcony or a mountain overlook?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the big thing is definitely on job sites. Like when I’m near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or one of those upper platforms, it just hits me — my head tells me I’m about to go over. And I can be looking right at the rails, knowing they’re in place, knowing the setup is supposed to be safe because that’s literally part of my job to understand… but it doesn’t really calm me down.\n\nI don’t know, balconies and that kind of thing… I guess I think of the same sort of feeling, but work is where it’s been really in my face. Like I’ll be standing there trying to focus on the crew or the plan for the day, and suddenly all I can think is, “I’m going to fall.”\n\nDoctor: Does this fear of falling happen to you when you're not working, like when you're with family or friends in a high place?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, it’s the same kind of thing, but what’s really been happening is at work. Every time I have to get near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or one of those upper platforms, my head just goes straight to, like, “you’re going to go over.”\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds strange because I can see the rails are there. I know they’re supposed to be safe — I’ve inspected that stuff myself. I’ve been doing this long enough to know what’s solid and what isn’t. But it doesn’t matter much in the moment. It just hits me like I’m about to fall, even when nothing’s actually wrong.\n\nWith family or friends… I guess if it’s a high place, I’d be thinking about it, but the main thing is the job sites. That’s where it’s been hard to get around.\n\nDoctor: Is this fear of falling ever present when you are in high places and not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess if I’m up high anywhere, my mind can go there, but the main place it really shows up is work. Like, every time I have to get near a scaffold or a roof edge or one of those upper platforms, it’s like my head just starts yelling that I’m about to go over.\n\nAnd I know that sounds ridiculous because I can see the rails right there. I know the setup is safe — or supposed to be safe — I mean, I’ve been the guy checking that kind of thing. It’s funny, not funny really, but I can tell a crew the platform’s secure and then my own brain won’t believe it when I’m the one standing there. It just… doesn’t matter what I know in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you're in a high place, not thinking about work, and you don't experience this fear of falling?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m trying to think of a time where it doesn’t happen… and I’m kind of coming up blank. Like, even if it’s not work exactly, the second I step onto temporary stairs or even just walk toward an open upper floor, my chest tightens up. My hands get wet, and I feel like my legs might lock before I can make myself take another step.\n\nIt’s weird because I’ll be telling myself, “Just move, it’s fine,” but my body’s already reacting. And then I start thinking about dumb stuff, like how I used to run up stairs without even thinking about it, or how other guys on site just walk around like nothing’s happening. But for me, once I’m up there or heading toward that edge, it just kicks in.\n\nDoctor: Is this fear of falling from heights something that you've experienced at any point in your life before you started working in construction?\n\nPatient: No… not really before that. I mean, I wasn’t someone who loved heights or anything, but I could do the job. I could be up where I needed to be.\n\nThis really started after the stair tower accident, about… thirteen months ago now. Funny how I can remember that so clearly, but I’ll forget where I put my keys half the time. But yeah, before that accident, this wasn’t like this.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of falling something that you experience in your dreams or when you're not actively thinking about work or being in high places?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, dreams are hard for me to pin down—half the time I wake up and it’s gone, like when you walk into a room and forget why you went in there.\n\nWhen I’m not thinking about work or being up high, I don’t know that I’ve noticed it showing up on its own. It’s more like… I only really recognize it when something puts heights in my head. But I haven’t exactly been keeping track, you know?\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of falling from heights something that affects your personal relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, outside of work-work, it’s mostly the daily stuff around the job, if that makes sense. Like I’ll put off an upper-level walk-through, or I’ll send somebody else up first, and then suddenly everyone’s looking at me like, “Why are you making that call if you haven’t been up there yourself?”\n\nAnd crews talk, you know. Subcontractors too. One guy gives you a look and then it’s like being back in high school shop class—everybody knows something’s off. I’ve delayed enough of those inspections and walk-throughs that people have started questioning me, and that’s… that’s not great in my position.\n\nIt got bad enough that my employer’s insurer pushed for an occupational health review because I wasn’t getting some of the key safety inspections done. So yeah, it’s affecting things. Maybe not like dinner with my family or going to the grocery store, but my day-to-day and relationships with people I work with, definitely.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of falling from heights something that you experience when you are alone, not at work, and not thinking about job-related tasks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really caught myself feeling it just sitting at home or anything like that.\n\nLike if I’m alone, my mind usually goes to chores or bills or whatever’s broken around the house—there’s always something. Last week it was the water heater making that knocking sound, and then I got pulled into looking up parts for it for two hours. Like, if there’s no work tied to it and no actual height involved… I don’t really notice it showing up.\n\nI mean, I’ll get bothered if something makes me think about being up high, but that’s kind of the point, right? It’s connected to heights somehow. If I’m just at home or doing normal stuff—taking the trash out, watching TV, fixing something in the garage—I can’t say it just pops into my head on its own.\n\nUnless I’m misunderstanding what you mean by “absolutely no connection.”\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a situation where you were in a high place, not at work, and not thinking about work, and you still experienced this fear of falling?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… if I’m being honest, it’s mostly when I’m near something like a scaffold, a roof edge, or an upper platform. That’s when it hits me.\n\nEven if I can look right at it and see the rails are there, and I know—like logically I know—it’s supposed to be safe, my head just goes, “You’re about to go over.” It’s stupid, because I’ve spent years around that kind of setup, you know? I can tell when something’s built right. I’ve corrected guys on rail heights and tie-offs and all that.\n\nBut now when I get near the edge, work or not, my brain doesn’t care. It just tells me I’m going to fall.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any recent situation where you were in a high place, not at work, and not thinking about work, and you still felt the fear of falling?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, the recent ones all kind of blur together because it’s the same thing every time I get near something high. Like a roof edge, or one of those upper platforms, or even just seeing a scaffold set up somewhere.\n\nI know that sounds work-related, but it’s not always me working. It’s more like if I’m near it, my head just flips into this thing where it tells me I’m about to go over. Even if I can see the rails are there, even if I know it’s set up right and it should be safe.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I understand that stuff better than most people. I’ll be looking at it thinking, “That’s fine, that’s built right,” and then my body—or my brain, I guess—just doesn’t care. It’s like, “Nope, you’re going to fall.”\n\n[Last Question]\nIs there anything about your fear of falling that you think is specifically related to your job as a construction manager, rather than just being in high places in general?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {

    "content": "patient_turn_index: 13\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."

(Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).","reason":"The doctor is asking whether the fear is tied to the specific phobic situation of being in high places, such as scaffolds or roof edges, versus being specifically job-related."}

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess the job part is that those are the places I’m around all the time—scaffolds, roof edges, upper platforms, that kind of thing. So it’s hard to separate it, you know?

But the fear itself… it’s not like I’m worried about paperwork or managing the crew or anything like that. It’s more that every time I have to get near one of those spots now, my head tells me I’m about to go over. Even when I can see the rails are there, and I know the setup is supposed to be safe, I still get that feeling.

I mean, I know construction sites, I know what’s safe and what isn’t—or I used to feel like I did. So yeah, it’s related to my job because my job puts me near those places, but it feels like the actual fear is the high place itself.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I’m here because work’s gotten kind of hard to deal with. I’m a construction manager, so I’m around job sites all the time, and anytime I have to get near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or one of those upper platforms, my head just starts telling me I’m about to go over.\n\nAnd the thing is, I can see the rails are there. I know the setup’s supposed to be safe — I mean, I’ve checked those things myself plenty of times. I know how it’s built. But it doesn’t seem to matter. I get up there and my mind just goes straight to, “You’re going over.” It’s… it’s been getting to where I don’t know how I’m supposed to do my job like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this fear of falling when you're in other high places outside of work, like a balcony or a mountain overlook?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the big thing is definitely on job sites. Like when I’m near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or one of those upper platforms, it just hits me — my head tells me I’m about to go over. And I can be looking right at the rails, knowing they’re in place, knowing the setup is supposed to be safe because that’s literally part of my job to understand… but it doesn’t really calm me down.\n\nI don’t know, balconies and that kind of thing… I guess I think of the same sort of feeling, but work is where it’s been really in my face. Like I’ll be standing there trying to focus on the crew or the plan for the day, and suddenly all I can think is, “I’m going to fall.”\n\nDoctor: Does this fear of falling happen to you when you're not working, like when you're with family or friends in a high place?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, it’s the same kind of thing, but what’s really been happening is at work. Every time I have to get near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or one of those upper platforms, my head just goes straight to, like, “you’re going to go over.”\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds strange because I can see the rails are there. I know they’re supposed to be safe — I’ve inspected that stuff myself. I’ve been doing this long enough to know what’s solid and what isn’t. But it doesn’t matter much in the moment. It just hits me like I’m about to fall, even when nothing’s actually wrong.\n\nWith family or friends… I guess if it’s a high place, I’d be thinking about it, but the main thing is the job sites. That’s where it’s been hard to get around.\n\nDoctor: Is this fear of falling ever present when you are in high places and not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess if I’m up high anywhere, my mind can go there, but the main place it really shows up is work. Like, every time I have to get near a scaffold or a roof edge or one of those upper platforms, it’s like my head just starts yelling that I’m about to go over.\n\nAnd I know that sounds ridiculous because I can see the rails right there. I know the setup is safe — or supposed to be safe — I mean, I’ve been the guy checking that kind of thing. It’s funny, not funny really, but I can tell a crew the platform’s secure and then my own brain won’t believe it when I’m the one standing there. It just… doesn’t matter what I know in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you're in a high place, not thinking about work, and you don't experience this fear of falling?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m trying to think of a time where it doesn’t happen… and I’m kind of coming up blank. Like, even if it’s not work exactly, the second I step onto temporary stairs or even just walk toward an open upper floor, my chest tightens up. My hands get wet, and I feel like my legs might lock before I can make myself take another step.\n\nIt’s weird because I’ll be telling myself, “Just move, it’s fine,” but my body’s already reacting. And then I start thinking about dumb stuff, like how I used to run up stairs without even thinking about it, or how other guys on site just walk around like nothing’s happening. But for me, once I’m up there or heading toward that edge, it just kicks in.\n\nDoctor: Is this fear of falling from heights something that you've experienced at any point in your life before you started working in construction?\n\nPatient: No… not really before that. I mean, I wasn’t someone who loved heights or anything, but I could do the job. I could be up where I needed to be.\n\nThis really started after the stair tower accident, about… thirteen months ago now. Funny how I can remember that so clearly, but I’ll forget where I put my keys half the time. But yeah, before that accident, this wasn’t like this.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of falling something that you experience in your dreams or when you're not actively thinking about work or being in high places?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, dreams are hard for me to pin down—half the time I wake up and it’s gone, like when you walk into a room and forget why you went in there.\n\nWhen I’m not thinking about work or being up high, I don’t know that I’ve noticed it showing up on its own. It’s more like… I only really recognize it when something puts heights in my head. But I haven’t exactly been keeping track, you know?\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of falling from heights something that affects your personal relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, outside of work-work, it’s mostly the daily stuff around the job, if that makes sense. Like I’ll put off an upper-level walk-through, or I’ll send somebody else up first, and then suddenly everyone’s looking at me like, “Why are you making that call if you haven’t been up there yourself?”\n\nAnd crews talk, you know. Subcontractors too. One guy gives you a look and then it’s like being back in high school shop class—everybody knows something’s off. I’ve delayed enough of those inspections and walk-throughs that people have started questioning me, and that’s… that’s not great in my position.\n\nIt got bad enough that my employer’s insurer pushed for an occupational health review because I wasn’t getting some of the key safety inspections done. So yeah, it’s affecting things. Maybe not like dinner with my family or going to the grocery store, but my day-to-day and relationships with people I work with, definitely.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of falling from heights something that you experience when you are alone, not at work, and not thinking about job-related tasks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really caught myself feeling it just sitting at home or anything like that.\n\nLike if I’m alone, my mind usually goes to chores or bills or whatever’s broken around the house—there’s always something. Last week it was the water heater making that knocking sound, and then I got pulled into looking up parts for it for two hours. So, no, I can’t say I notice the fear just coming out of nowhere when I’m not thinking about heights or work stuff. At least not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Does this fear of falling ever occur to you in situations that have absolutely no connection to your job or work-related activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve got a clean example of that. Like, if there’s no work tied to it and no actual height involved… I don’t really notice it showing up.\n\nI mean, I’ll get bothered if something makes me think about being up high, but that’s kind of the point, right? It’s connected to heights somehow. If I’m just at home or doing normal stuff—taking the trash out, watching TV, fixing something in the garage—I can’t say it just pops into my head on its own.\n\nUnless I’m misunderstanding what you mean by “absolutely no connection.”\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a situation where you were in a high place, not at work, and not thinking about work, and you still experienced this fear of falling?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… if I’m being honest, it’s mostly when I’m near something like a scaffold, a roof edge, or an upper platform. That’s when it hits me.\n\nEven if I can look right at it and see the rails are there, and I know—like logically I know—it’s supposed to be safe, my head just goes, “You’re about to go over.” It’s stupid, because I’ve spent years around that kind of setup, you know? I can tell when something’s built right. I’ve corrected guys on rail heights and tie-offs and all that.\n\nBut now when I get near the edge, work or not, my brain doesn’t care. It just tells me I’m going to fall.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any recent situation where you were in a high place, not at work, and not thinking about work, and you still felt the fear of falling?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, the recent ones all kind of blur together because it’s the same thing every time I get near something high. Like a roof edge, or one of those upper platforms, or even just seeing a scaffold set up somewhere.\n\nI know that sounds work-related, but it’s not always me working. It’s more like if I’m near it, my head just flips into this thing where it tells me I’m about to go over. Even if I can see the rails are there, even if I know it’s set up right and it should be safe.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I understand that stuff better than most people. I’ll be looking at it thinking, “That’s fine, that’s built right,” and then my body—or my brain, I guess—just doesn’t care. It’s like, “Nope, you’re going to fall.”\n\nDoctor: Is there anything about your fear of falling that you think is specifically related to your job as a construction manager, rather than just being in high places in general?\n\nPatient: I guess the job part is that those are the places I’m around all the time—scaffolds, roof edges, upper platforms, that kind of thing. So it’s hard to separate it, you know?\n\nBut the fear itself… it’s not like I’m worried about paperwork or managing the crew or anything like that. It’s more that every time I have to get near one of those spots now, my head tells me I’m about to go over. Even when I can see the rails are there, and I know the setup is supposed to be safe, I still get that feeling.\n\nI mean, I know construction sites, I know what’s safe and what isn’t—or I used to feel like I did. So yeah, it’s related to my job because my job puts me near those places, but it feels like the actual fear is the high place itself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

If I’m just at home or doing normal stuff—taking the trash out, watching TV, fixing something in the garage—I can’t say it just pops into my head on its own.\n\nUnless I’m misunderstanding what you mean by “absolutely no connection.”\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a situation where you were in a high place, not at work, and not thinking about work, and you still experienced this fear of falling?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… if I’m being honest, it’s mostly when I’m near something like a scaffold, a roof edge, or an upper platform. That’s when it hits me.\n\nEven if I can look right at it and see the rails are there, and I know—like logically I know—it’s supposed to be safe, my head just goes, “You’re about to go over.” It’s stupid, because I’ve spent years around that kind of setup, you know? It just tells me I’m going to fall.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any recent situation where you were in a high place, not at work, and not thinking about work, and you still felt the fear of falling?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, the recent ones all kind of blur together because it’s the same thing every time I get near something high. So it’s hard to separate it, you know?\n\nBut the fear itself… it’s not like I’m worried about paperwork or managing the crew or anything like that. I’m trying to think of one that’s totally separate, and… honestly it always comes back to the same kind of places. Like a scaffold, a roof edge, an upper platform—whether I’m there for work or not, that’s what sets it off.

It’s not that I’m standing there thinking about being a construction manager. I can look at the rails and know they’re in place, know the setup should be safe, and still my head just goes, “You’re going to go over.” And then I get stuck on that.

I know that doesn’t exactly answer it cleanly, but that’s the thing—it’s the height and the edge, not really the job title.

From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF40.228, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I’m here because work’s gotten kind of hard to deal with. I’m a construction manager, so I’m around job sites all the time, and anytime I have to get near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or one of those upper platforms, my head just starts telling me I’m about to go over.\n\nAnd the thing is, I can see the rails are there. I know the setup’s supposed to be safe — I mean, I’ve checked those things myself plenty of times. I know how it’s built. But it doesn’t seem to matter. I get up there and my mind just goes straight to, “You’re going over.” It’s… it’s been getting to where I don’t know how I’m supposed to do my job like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this fear of falling when you're in other high places outside of work, like a balcony or a mountain overlook?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the big thing is definitely on job sites. Like when I’m near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or one of those upper platforms, it just hits me — my head tells me I’m about to go over. And I can be looking right at the rails, knowing they’re in place, knowing the setup is supposed to be safe because that’s literally part of my job to understand… but it doesn’t really calm me down.\n\nI don’t know, balconies and that kind of thing… I guess I think of the same sort of feeling, but work is where it’s been really in my face. Like I’ll be standing there trying to focus on the crew or the plan for the day, and suddenly all I can think is, “I’m going to fall.”\n\nDoctor: Does this fear of falling happen to you when you're not working, like when you're with family or friends in a high place?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, it’s the same kind of thing, but what’s really been happening is at work. Every time I have to get near a scaffold, or a roof edge, or one of those upper platforms, my head just goes straight to, like, “you’re going to go over.”\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds strange because I can see the rails are there. I know they’re supposed to be safe — I’ve inspected that stuff myself. I’ve been doing this long enough to know what’s solid and what isn’t. But it doesn’t matter much in the moment. It just hits me like I’m about to fall, even when nothing’s actually wrong.\n\nWith family or friends… I guess if it’s a high place, I’d be thinking about it, but the main thing is the job sites. That’s where it’s been hard to get around.\n\nDoctor: Is this fear of falling ever present when you are in high places and not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess if I’m up high anywhere, my mind can go there, but the main place it really shows up is work. Like, every time I have to get near a scaffold or a roof edge or one of those upper platforms, it’s like my head just starts yelling that I’m about to go over.\n\nAnd I know that sounds ridiculous because I can see the rails right there. I know the setup is safe — or supposed to be safe — I mean, I’ve been the guy checking that kind of thing. It’s funny, not funny really, but I can tell a crew the platform’s secure and then my own brain won’t believe it when I’m the one standing there. It just… doesn’t matter what I know in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you're in a high place, not thinking about work, and you don't experience this fear of falling?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m trying to think of a time where it doesn’t happen… and I’m kind of coming up blank. Like, even if it’s not work exactly, the second I step onto temporary stairs or even just walk toward an open upper floor, my chest tightens up. My hands get wet, and I feel like my legs might lock before I can make myself take another step.\n\nIt’s weird because I’ll be telling myself, “Just move, it’s fine,” but my body’s already reacting. And then I start thinking about dumb stuff, like how I used to run up stairs without even thinking about it, or how other guys on site just walk around like nothing’s happening. But for me, once I’m up there or heading toward that edge, it just kicks in.\n\nDoctor: Is this fear of falling from heights something that you've experienced at any point in your life before you started working in construction?\n\nPatient: No… not really before that. I mean, I wasn’t someone who loved heights or anything, but I could do the job. I could be up where I needed to be.\n\nThis really started after the stair tower accident, about… thirteen months ago now. Funny how I can remember that so clearly, but I’ll forget where I put my keys half the time. But yeah, before that accident, this wasn’t like this.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of falling something that you experience in your dreams or when you're not actively thinking about work or being in high places?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, dreams are hard for me to pin down—half the time I wake up and it’s gone, like when you walk into a room and forget why you went in there.\n\nWhen I’m not thinking about work or being up high, I don’t know that I’ve noticed it showing up on its own. It’s more like… I only really recognize it when something puts heights in my head. But I haven’t exactly been keeping track, you know?\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of falling from heights something that affects your personal relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, outside of work-work, it’s mostly the daily stuff around the job, if that makes sense. Like I’ll put off an upper-level walk-through, or I’ll send somebody else up first, and then suddenly everyone’s looking at me like, “Why are you making that call if you haven’t been up there yourself?”\n\nAnd crews talk, you know. Subcontractors too. One guy gives you a look and then it’s like being back in high school shop class—everybody knows something’s off. I’ve delayed enough of those inspections and walk-throughs that people have started questioning me, and that’s… that’s not great in my position.\n\nIt got bad enough that my employer’s insurer pushed for an occupational health review because I wasn’t getting some of the key safety inspections done. So yeah, it’s affecting things. Maybe not like dinner with my family or going to the grocery store, but my day-to-day and relationships with people I work with, definitely.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of falling from heights something that you experience when you are alone, not at work, and not thinking about job-related tasks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really caught myself feeling it just sitting at home or anything like that.\n\nLike if I’m alone, my mind usually goes to chores or bills or whatever’s broken around the house—there’s always something. Last week it was the water heater making that knocking sound, and then I got pulled into looking up parts for it for two hours. So, no, I can’t say I notice the fear just coming out of nowhere when I’m not thinking about heights or work stuff. At least not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Does this fear of falling ever occur to you in situations that have absolutely no connection to your job or work-related activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve got a clean example of that. Like, if there’s no work tied to it and no actual height involved… I don’t really notice it showing up.\n\nI mean, I’ll get bothered if something makes me think about being up high, but that’s kind of the point, right? It’s connected to heights somehow. If I’m just at home or doing normal stuff—taking the trash out, watching TV, fixing something in the garage—I can’t say it just pops into my head on its own.\n\nUnless I’m misunderstanding what you mean by “absolutely no connection.”\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a situation where you were in a high place, not at work, and not thinking about work, and you still experienced this fear of falling?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… if I’m being honest, it’s mostly when I’m near something like a scaffold, a roof edge, or an upper platform. That’s when it hits me.\n\nEven if I can look right at it and see the rails are there, and I know—like logically I know—it’s supposed to be safe, my head just goes, “You’re about to go over.” It’s stupid, because I’ve spent years around that kind of setup, you know? I can tell when something’s built right. I’ve corrected guys on rail heights and tie-offs and all that.\n\nBut now when I get near the edge, work or not, my brain doesn’t care. It just tells me I’m going to fall.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any recent situation where you were in a high place, not at work, and not thinking about work, and you still felt the fear of falling?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, the recent ones all kind of blur together because it’s the same thing every time I get near something high. Like a roof edge, or one of those upper platforms, or even just seeing a scaffold set up somewhere.\n\nI know that sounds work-related, but it’s not always me working. It’s more like if I’m near it, my head just flips into this thing where it tells me I’m about to go over. Even if I can see the rails are there, even if I know it’s set up right and it should be safe.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I understand that stuff better than most people. I’ll be looking at it thinking, “That’s fine, that’s built right,” and then my body—or my brain, I guess—just doesn’t care. It’s like, “Nope, you’re going to fall.”\n\nDoctor: Is there anything about your fear of falling that you think is specifically related to your job as a construction manager, rather than just being in high places in general?\n\nPatient: I guess the job part is that those are the places I’m around all the time—scaffolds, roof edges, upper platforms, that kind of thing. So it’s hard to separate it, you know?\n\nBut the fear itself… it’s not like I’m worried about paperwork or managing the crew or anything like that. It’s more that every time I have to get near one of those spots now, my head tells me I’m about to go over. Even when I can see the rails are there, and I know the setup is supposed to be safe, I still get that feeling.\n\nI mean, I know construction sites, I know what’s safe and what isn’t—or I used to feel like I did. So yeah, it’s related to my job because my job puts me near those places, but it feels like the actual fear is the high place itself.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any situation where you felt the fear of falling when you were in a high place that had nothing to do with your job as a construction manager?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one that’s totally separate, and… honestly it always comes back to the same kind of places. Like a scaffold, a roof edge, an upper platform—whether I’m there for work or not, that’s what sets it off.\n\nIt’s not that I’m standing there thinking about being a construction manager. I can look at the rails and know they’re in place, know the setup should be safe, and still my head just goes, “You’re going to go over.” And then I get stuck on that.\n\nI know that doesn’t exactly answer it cleanly, but that’s the thing—it’s the height and the edge, not really the job title.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
